<p><span style="font-size: 10pt;">Table 4. LANDFALL C2 servers.</span></p>
<h2><a id="post-164365-_8ihfzy80sg4e"></a>How LANDFALL Fits Into the Larger Picture</h2>
<p>LANDFALL is one example of a larger pattern of exploit chains affecting mobile devices, related to DNG image processing vulnerabilities.</p>
<p>The LANDFALL campaign's use of a malformed DNG file highlights a significant, recurring attack vector: the targeting of vulnerabilities within DNG image processing libraries. The specific flaw LANDFALL exploited, CVE-2025-21042, is not an isolated case but rather part of a broader pattern of similar issues found on multiple mobile platforms. In fact, earlier in 2025, Samsung identified another DNG flaw in the same Samsung library, CVE-2025-21043, and the parallel exploit chain on iOS was identified that leveraged CVE-2025-43300 in Apple iOS and CVE-2025-55177 in WhatsApp.</p>
<h3><a id="post-164365-_jcu0rbgg0cty"></a>Relationship to CVE-2025-21043 (SVE-2025-1702)</h3>
<p>Our analysis revealed a possible connection to a separate vulnerability in the same library, <a href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-21043" target="_blank" rel="noopener">CVE-2025-21043</a> (SVE-2025-1702), which Samsung patched in its September 2025 security update. While it was not exploited in the LANDFALL samples we discovered, the similarities between the exploit for LANDFALL (CVE-2025-21042) and this vulnerability (CVE-2025-21043) are striking. Both vulnerabilities were publicly disclosed around the same time and both are connected to DNG image file processing delivered through mobile communication applications.</p>
<h3><a id="post-164365-_k38d0qyl62va"></a>Apple's CVE-2025-43300</h3>
<p>In August 2025, Apple addressed CVE-2025-43300, a zero-day vulnerability impacting DNG image parsing, which was actively exploited in the wild, to enable zero-click remote code execution through malicious images sent via mobile communication applications.</p>
<p>We cannot confirm whether this chain was used to deliver an equivalent of LANDFALL to iOS, or whether it is the same threat actor behind the two. However, this parallel development in the iOS ecosystem, combined with the disclosure of the Samsung and Apple vulnerabilities just a few weeks apart, highlights a broader pattern of DNG image processing vulnerabilities being leveraged in sophisticated mobile spyware attacks.</p>
<figure id="attachment_164432" aria-describedby="caption-attachment-164432" style="width: 1000px" class="wp-caption alignnone"><img  class="wp-image-164432 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-902272-164365-6.png" alt="Timeline graphic showing major cybersecurity events from 2024 to 2025 involving entities such as VirusTotal, Samsung, Apple, and WhatsApp. Key events include the discovery of a malicious DNG file on VirusTotal in July 2024 and various updates and patches by Samsung and Apple in response to different vulnerabilities. The timeline is from July 2024 to September 2025. " width="1000" height="818" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-902272-164365-6.png 2048w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-902272-164365-6-538x440.png 538w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-902272-164365-6-856x700.png 856w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-902272-164365-6-768x628.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/word-image-902272-164365-6-1536x1256.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 1000px) 100vw, 1000px" /><figcaption id="caption-attachment-164432" class="wp-caption-text">Figure 6. Timeline for recent malicious DNG image files and associated exploit activity.</figcaption></figure>
<ul>
<li><strong>July 2024 – February 2025:</strong> Initial samples of malicious DNG image files carrying LANDFALL are first submitted on VirusTotal in July 2024, with additional samples appearing periodically over the next several months.
<ul>
<li>The DNG files exploit a vulnerability in Samsung’s Android image processing library (SVE-2024-1969, CVE-2025-21042)</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>Sept. 25, 2024:</strong> The vulnerability is <a href="https://security.samsungmobile.com/securityUpdate.smsb#:~:text=Reported%20on%3A%20September%2025%2C%202024" target="_blank" rel="noopener">privately reported to Samsung</a>.</li>
<li><strong>April 2025: </strong>Samsung <a href="https://security.samsungmobile.com/securityUpdate.smsb?year=2025&amp;month=04" target="_blank" rel="noopener">issues a firmware update</a> to address the vulnerability, SVE-2024-1969, later known as CVE-2025-21042 when publicly disclosed.</li>
<li><strong>August 2025:</strong> Parallel developments occur.
<ul>
<li>Apple patches a zero-day vulnerability impacting DNG image parsing, which was actively exploited in the wild (CVE-2025-43300)</li>
<li>WhatsApp discloses a vulnerability (CVE-2025-55177) that was chained with Apple’s DNG image parsing zero-day vulnerability (CVE-2025-43300)</li>
<li>We discovered DNG image files exploiting CVE-2025-21042 to deliver Android spyware that we identified as LANDFALL.</li>
<li>WhatsApp disclosed to Samsung <a href="https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-21043" target="_blank" rel="noopener">CVE-2025-21043</a> — another DNG-related zero-day vulnerability in Samsung Galaxy devices.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><strong>September 2025:</strong> Samsung issues mobile device firmware updates for CVE-2025-21043 (SVE-2025-1702). Concurrently, it assigns CVE-2025-21042 (SVE-20254-1969) to the earlier vulnerability that previously had no CVE designator.</li>
</ul>
<h2><a id="post-164365-_xhh4m7ympgpe"></a>Potential Victims</h2>
<p>Analysis of VirusTotal submission data for the malicious DNG files indicates potential targets in Iraq, Iran, Turkey and Morocco.</p>
<p>Turkey's national CERT (in Turkish, USOM) reported <a href="https://www.usom.gov.tr/adres/946134" target="_blank" rel="noopener">IP addresses used by LANDFALL's C2 servers</a> as malicious, mobile- and APT-related, which also supports the possible targeting of victims in Turkey.</p>
<h2><a id="post-164365-_lnwgtub4gap0"></a>Relationship to Known Spyware Groups</h2>
<p>While we were unable to recover every component of the LANDFALL framework, it is clear that the tool is commercial grade. It may have utilized several zero-day exploits in its infection chain.</p>
<p>Such tools are often developed and sold as commercial spyware and attributed to groups known as private sector offensive actors (PSOAs), who are often legitimate legal entities. Reportedly, these groups provide services to government entities.</p>
<p>We were not able at this time to <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/unit-42-attribution-framework/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">officially attribute</a> LANDFALL activity to a known PSOA or threat actor. Unit 42 tracks the activity related to CVE-2025-21042 and LANDFALL as CL-UNK-1054.</p>
<p>Two aspects are notable and worth highlighting.</p>
<p>First, LANDFALL's C2 infrastructure and domain registration patterns share similarities to infrastructure associated with <a href="https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0038/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Stealth Falcon</a> as observed by Unit 42. These similarities are based on various <a href="https://research.checkpoint.com/2025/stealth-falcon-zero-day/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">public</a> <a href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/stealth-falcon-preying-middle-eastern-skies-deadglyph/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">reports</a>, as well as Stealth Falcon activity we have analyzed for targets in the Middle East.</p>
<p>Second, in its own debug artifacts, the spyware component we analyzed refers to itself as “Bridge Head.” Of note, the term Bridge Head is a common nickname used by some private-sector offensive cyber companies (including <a href="https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/research/2021/07/forensic-methodology-report-how-to-catch-nso-groups-pegasus/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">NSO</a>, <a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-uniblog-publish-prod/documents/Buying_Spying_-_Insights_into_Commercial_Surveillance_Vendors_-_TAG_report.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Variston [PDF]</a>, Cytrox and Quadream) for first-stage loaders. However, this naming convention alone does not constitute a direct attribution link.</p>
<p>While this is a common name used in commercial mobile spyware to describe loaders, it draws similarities to the Heliconica framework. This framework also contains references to “BridgeHead,” as <a href="https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-uniblog-publish-prod/documents/Buying_Spying_-_Insights_into_Commercial_Surveillance_Vendors_-_TAG_report.pdf?#page=22" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Google TAG reported</a> about spyware vendor Variston. <a href="https://blog.google/threat-analysis-group/new-details-on-commercial-spyware-vendor-variston/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Google identified</a> Variston as a Barcelona-based PSOA (provider of exploits). Further analysis from Google and <a href="https://techcrunch.com/2024/02/15/variston-spyware-losing-staff-some-say-closing/#:~:text=%E2%80%9CVariston%20was%20a,former%20Variston%20employees" target="_blank" rel="noopener">other reports</a> indicated Variston's tooling was supplied to clients in the UAE through a reseller named Protect Electronic Systems (or Protected AE).</p>
<p>This potential provider-client link to the UAE is noteworthy, as <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/unified-secops/microsoft-threat-actor-naming#:~:text=Daffodil%20Gust,Armor%2C%20Project%20Raven" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Microsoft</a> and <a href="https://apt.etda.or.th/cgi-bin/showcard.cgi?g=Stealth%20Falcon%2C%20FruityArmor#:~:text=Country-,UAE,-Motivation" target="_blank" rel="noopener">others</a> reported that Stealth Falcon also operates heavily out of that country. Variston <a href="https://techcrunch.com/2025/02/13/barcelona-based-spyware-startup-variston-reportedly-shuts-down/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">reportedly ceased operations</a> in early 2025 following its public exposure.</p>
<p>As of October 2025, except in infrastructure, we have not observed direct overlaps between the mobile campaigns of LANDFALL and the endpoint-based activity from Stealth Falcon, nor direct strong links with Stealth Falcon. However, the similarities are worth discussion.</p>
<h2><a id="post-164365-_e7bo8fcaslx9"></a>Conclusion</h2>
<p>The discovery of LANDFALL spyware reveals a campaign targeting Samsung Android devices. The exploit chain involves CVE-2025-21042, a vulnerability that was patched by Samsung in April 2025. The presence of this spyware within DNG image files with WhatsApp-related naming conventions likely indicates attackers attempted to deliver the exploit through a messaging application.</p>
<p>From the initial appearance of samples in July 2024, this activity highlights how sophisticated exploits can remain in public repositories for an extended period before being fully understood.</p>
<p>The analysis of the loader reveals evidence of commercial-grade activity. The LANDFALL spyware components suggest advanced capabilities for stealth, persistence and comprehensive data collection from modern Samsung devices.</p>
<p>However, we have not directly analyzed the next-stage components of the spyware. Additional details on this or on the exact delivery method would provide even more insight into the malicious activity.</p>
<p>Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from LANDFALL Android spyware through the following products:</p>
<ul>
<li>The <a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/wildfire" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced WildFire</a> machine-learning models and analysis techniques have been reviewed and updated in light of the indicators shared in this research.</li>
<li><a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/advanced-url-filtering/administration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced URL Filtering</a> and <a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/dns-security" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced DNS Security</a> identify known domains and URLs associated with this activity as malicious.</li>
<li><a href="https://docs.paloaltonetworks.com/advanced-threat-prevention/administration" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Advanced Threat Prevention</a> has an inbuilt machine learning-based detection that can detect exploits in real time.</li>
</ul>

<h2><a id="post-164365-_tu2h38821ats"></a>Appendices</h2>
<h3><a id="post-164365-_qmjmlhzgwvpl"></a>Appendix A: SELinux Policy Manipulation</h3>
<p>LANDFALL's component for SELinux policy manipulation is <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">l.so</span>. This file provides a capability to bypass system security controls. It is decompressed from <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/l</span> to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/l.so</span> and executed.</p>
<p>Rather than containing hard-coded rules, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">l.so</span> implements a generic engine that can dynamically parse and load new SELinux policy statements from an external source, modifying the running policy in memory.</p>
<p>Relevant and unique exported functions:</p>
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sepolicy_from_data</span>: Load policy from binary data</li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sepolicy_add_statement</span>: Add individual policy statements</li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sepolicy_to_buffer</span>: Serialize modified policy</li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sepolicy_delete</span>: Clean up policy objects</li>
</ul>
<h3><a id="post-164365-_6k6k0f66tz4g"></a>Appendix B: Additional Details on LANDFALL Spyware Analysis</h3>
<p>This appendix details the observed capabilities of the loader component of LANDFALL, as well as those we infer exist in other modules of the complete LANDFALL framework that we have not yet accessed.</p>
<p>LANDFALL’s Bridge Head, named on the disk as <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">b.so</span>, is loaded by an exploit on the device. Immediately after being loaded post‑exploit, LANDFALL parses <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">LD_PRELOAD</span> from the environment to avoid inheriting upstream preloads. It reads the effective user ID via <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">geteuid()</span> and stores it globally so later branches can adjust behavior for root versus non‑root. Then it calls into the main routine.</p>
<p>It gathers process basics (parent <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">pid</span>, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">euid</span>, Android build string), reads a <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">runner</span> flag from the environment variable <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">R</span> and takes a copy of it for later actions. This value (typically <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">I</span> for interactive or <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">P</span> for passive) will be reported to the command and control and determine how it launches a later staged payload. It resolves its own mapped path, selects the app-private base at <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/</span> as its working directory and then constructs two child paths there. One path is for the staged download and one is for the final <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">l.so</span> used for execution.</p>
<h4><a id="post-164365-_wovjvqq8yprs"></a>Configuration</h4>
<p>LANDFALL reads and XOR-decrypts a JSON configuration directly from its own file. The spyware normalizes configuration by writing internal defaults back into the parsed object: numeric fields default when missing or zero, and certain booleans are coerced to fixed values regardless of the supplied configuration. Finally, it checks that a public key (X.509 DER) is present in the configuration and exits otherwise.</p>

<h4><a id="post-164365-_6c2o6pblm9qf"></a>Communication With the C2 Server</h4>
<p>Once configuration is complete, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">b.so</span> loads the necessary network and encryption functions, sets up its SSL environment, and prepares a secure client context for future HTTPS communication. If any part of this setup fails, it exits cleanly rather than risk a partial setup.</p>
<p>Before entering the C2 beaconing loop, it optionally emits telemetry. If it is running as root, it formats a concise "started" line that includes:</p>
<ul>
<li>A fixed version marker (e.g., <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">v1.5.0</span>)</li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">pid</span></li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">euid</span></li>
<li>Runner mode</li>
</ul>
<p>It then sends that via a POST request to the C2 server, as shown below.</p>
<p><img  class="alignnone wp-image-164480 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-1.png" alt="POST request to C2 server " width="800" height="115" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-1.png 1826w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-1-786x113.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-1-768x110.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-1-1536x220.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 800px) 100vw, 800px" /></p>
<p>If the runner mode is <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">I</span>, it prepares the SELinux helper first: it locates the compressed policy helper (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/l</span>), inflates it via an internal XZ decoder into a fresh buffer, and writes the result to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/l.so</span>. Then it beacons to the C2 server by sending ping using the hyphen‑stripped <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">agent_id</span> as payload. The outcome doesn’t gate the rest of the flow, and it serves as an operator telemetry.</p>
<p>An example of code for an <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">I</span>‑mode <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">ping</span> follows.</p>
<p><img  class="alignnone wp-image-164469 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image4.png" alt="Example of code for I-ping to host server." width="800" height="198" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image4.png 1852w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image4-786x194.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image4-768x190.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image4-1536x380.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 800px) 100vw, 800px" /></p>
<p>Only then does the main C2 beaconing loop begin. It records time zero and enforces a strict wall‑clock budget of about 7,200 seconds (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">suicide_time</span>) for the entire session.</p>
<p>It zeros two large, reusable path buffers: one for the raw blob that may arrive from the server, and a sibling for the decompressed shared object. Both paths live under the app‑private working directory (e.g., <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/</span>). With the paths ready, it contacts the command and control server by building a compact, URL‑encoded style body of <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">key=value</span> pairs:</p>
<ul>
<li>A protocol/variant tag; the beacon message type</li>
<li>The <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">agent_id</span>, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">command_id</span> and a fresh <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">upload_id</span> (UUIDv4) for correlation</li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">source=bridge_head</span></li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">Euid</span></li>
<li>The runner flag and its on‑disk install path</li>
</ul>
<p>When applicable, it computes a SHA-1 over part of the message and appends it. It uses the following Chrome User‑Agent string:</p>
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">Mozilla/5.0 (Macintosh; Intel Mac OS X 10_10_3) AppleWebKit/537.36 (KHTML, like Gecko) Chrome/44.0.2403.89 Safari/537.36</span></li>
</ul>
<p>Requests are directed at the configured base path (e.g., <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">POST /is/ HTTP/1.0</span>). Before any application data is sent, it pins the TLS connection.</p>
<p>While reading the response from the C2 server, LANDFALL checks the response status code and looks through the headers to determine the message length and type. Some status codes — particularly timeouts and a masked set of <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">4xx</span>/<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">5xx</span> (e.g., <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">408</span>, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">504</span>) — are treated as transient and trigger <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sleep(sleep_time_between_retries)</span> (default 35 seconds) before retrying with a fresh connection. A not‑found response (e.g., <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">404</span>) is considered terminal for this run and exits the loop. If the status indicates success and a body is present, LANDFALL proceeds to staging.</p>
<h4><a id="post-164365-_73jhoeobbek0"></a>Next-Stage Payloads</h4>
<p>Staging starts by cleaning up the file system state. If an old raw blob exists at the temporary path, it removes it. It writes the raw bytes to a staging file named <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">aa.so</span> under the working directory, and then, attempts to decompress them using a built‑in XZ/LZMA decoder that inflates into a pre‑allocated approximately 5 MiB buffer.</p>
<p>On success, it writes the decompressed bytes to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">dec_a.so</span> (the final <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">.so</span> path) under the working directory, clears the temporary <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">aa.so</span> and sets the file mode on <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">dec_a.so</span> to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">777</span> to ensure it’s loadable in various contexts.</p>
<p>Example XZ inflater (trimmed):</p>
<p><img  class="alignnone wp-image-164447 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image6.png" alt="Inflater that inflates to predetermined size. " width="700" height="233" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image6.png 1842w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image6-786x262.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image6-768x256.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image6-1536x512.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 700px) 100vw, 700px" /></p>
<p>With the shared object in place (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">dec_a.so</span>), it chooses an execution strategy based on the runner flag. For interactive setups (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">I</span>), it exports two environment variables and launches a benign system tool synchronously:</p>
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">LD_PRELOAD=&lt;final.so&gt; PRELOAD_PATH=/data/data/com.samsung.ipservice/files/l.so /system/bin/id (where &lt;final.so&gt; is dec_a.so)</span></li>
</ul>
<p>The <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">PRELOAD_PATH</span> points at the device path where SELinux policy content is expected, allowing the preloaded code to find and apply policy at startup. Note that this <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">PRELOAD_PATH</span> is only used in interactive mode, the passive running path omits it.</p>
<p>For passive runs (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">P</span>), it launches the same tool in the background:</p>
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">LD_PRELOAD=&lt;final.so&gt; /system/bin/id (with &lt;final.so&gt; is dec_a.so)</span></li>
</ul>
<p>This is done so control returns quickly while the helper initializes in another process. Internally, both are dispatched via a shell wrapper (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/system/bin/sh -c &lt;cmd&gt;</span>). In both cases, it accepts only narrow success results:</p>
<ul>
<li>exit code 0 or a specific <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x15</span>; anything else is treated as failure and breaks out of the loop</li>
</ul>
<p>On successful load, it formats and sends an “ended” line mirroring the opening message including:</p>
<ul>
<li>Version marker</li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">pid</span></li>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">​​incremental_build</span></li>
<li>runner</li>
</ul>
<p><img  class="alignnone wp-image-164458 lozad"  data-src="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9.png" alt="Code snippet " width="700" height="100" data-srcset="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9.png 1826w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-786x113.png 786w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-768x110.png 768w, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/image9-1536x220.png 1536w" sizes="(max-width: 700px) 100vw, 700px" /></p>
<p>It then frees transient strings and buffers. If no payload was available, or if a transient error occurred, it checks the elapsed wall‑clock time against its approximately 7,200‑second budget. If there’s time left, it sleeps the configured interval and tries again.</p>
<p>Finally, when the loop finishes, either after a successful loading of the next stage or due to time budget or unrecoverable errors, it unwinds cleanly. If it is running as root, it prefers a <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">direct _exit(status)</span> path instead of a normal return to minimize side effects in the runtime. In all cases, it aims to leave behind only the minimum artifacts needed for the staged code to continue.</p>
<h4><a id="post-164365-_q9filhj8cf9n"></a>Unreferenced Capabilities</h4>
<p>During reverse engineering, we identified multiple routines compiled into the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">b.so</span> component that are not invoked by its observed control flow. These latent features appear designed for use by the follow‑on modules loaded.</p>
<p>It is also very probable that some of these functions are leftovers from older versions of LANDFALL. They reveal concrete behaviors oriented around WhatsApp media paths, DCIM discovery, file system staging and process hygiene on Android:</p>
<ul>
<li>One routine prepares a “started” telemetry line and then interacts with the device’s media subsystem. It formats the line:
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">BH v1.5.0 started - pid: , euid=, incremental_build: v1.5.0, runner:</span></li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>If its internal checks pass, it executes a broadcast to force a gallery rescan using the exact shell:
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">am broadcast -a android.intent.action.MEDIA_SCANNER_SCAN_FILE -d file:///sdcard/DCIM/hacked.jpg</span></li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>In the same flow, it also constructs a “newest photo” probe over DCIM using:
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">find /sdcard/DCIM -type f -exec ls -t1 {} + | grep -v hacked| head -1</span></li>
<li>This pattern is consistent with harvesting the latest camera item while excluding an artifact it can plant. This routine is compiled in but not called by any other code in the sample.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>WhatsApp media path planter. Another routine decodes a hard-coded Base64 1x1 PNG (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">iVBORw0KGgoAAAANSUhEUgAAAAEAAAABCAYAAAAfFcSJ…JRU5ErkJggg==</span>) and searches WhatsApp’s media directories on external storage for a recent file path that matches the agent’s identifier (the UUID is first stripped of hyphens). It builds and executes a search pipeline across both default (ID 0) and multi‑user (ID 95) paths:
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">find /storage/emulated/0/Android/media/com.whatsapp/WhatsApp/Media/WhatsApp\ Images/ /storage/emulated/95/Android/media/com.whatsapp/WhatsApp/Media/WhatsApp\ Images/ -type f -atime -720m -maxdepth 1 -exec grep -lo '.*&lt;agentIdNoHyphens&gt;.*' {} \; -quit 2&gt;/dev/null</span>.</li>
<li>If such a path is returned, it writes the decoded PNG there verbatim. This looks like a cover‑artifact or covert marker stage aimed at WhatsApp images.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Another helper takes a base directory and a string and returns one matching JPEG path by executing:
<ul>
<li><span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">find &lt;base&gt; -name '*.jpg' -exec grep -le '.*&lt;pattern&gt;.*' {} \;</span></li>
<li>It trims trailing newlines and verifies the path exists before returning.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Zygote avoidance check: A process‑hygiene helper allocates a buffer for its own <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">cmdline</span> and returns success only when the name does not match <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">zygote</span> or <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">zygote64</span>. It is designed to avoid Android’s special host processes.</li>
<li>SELinux symbol resolver and cleanup: Two small routines handle dynamic SELinux plumbing.
<ul>
<li>One <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">dlopens /system/lib64/libselinux.so</span> and resolves <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">getfilecon</span> and <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">setfilecon</span> into global function pointers.</li>
<li>The other tears this down and clears the pointers.</li>
<li>Both exist to support <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">policy/file‑context</span> work but are not referenced by the observed code path.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>A more substantial routine accepts a list of file system paths. For each, it saves the current label via <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">getfilecon</span>, invokes an internal labeler on the path, applies ownership via <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">chown</span> and then restores the saved label with <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">setfilecon</span>. It returns distinct negative codes when <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">chown</span> or <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">setfilecon</span> fail.</li>
<li>There is a file probe that attempts to open a path and maps the outcome to internal status codes (success, permission denied, not found, generic error). It also resets internal library state (including any previously opened SELinux handles).</li>
<li>Map process‑execution outcome to message status: A tiny mapper converts the result of an internal command‑execution helper into message catalog codes (e.g., mapping a specific return (1) to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">CMD_STAT_*</span> code <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x0C</span> and 2–3 to <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x51</span>). It standardizes reporting for helpers but is not reached by the current logic.</li>
<li>Building a device‑report JSON array: Another dormant routine constructs a cJSON array where each entry carries <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">device_path</span>, a Base64‑encoded binary field, a <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">last_updated</span> boolean and a textual <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">state</span> derived from the internal <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">CMD_STAT_*</span> table. It walks an input vector, reads the referenced file into memory, Base64 encodes it and appends to the array.</li>
<li>A small string‑templating helper finds occurrences of the token <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">--working_dir--</span> inside a JSON value and replaces them with the runtime path tracked by the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">b.so</span>.</li>
<li>Appending <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">TracerPid</span> to telemetry: A diagnostic helper parses <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/proc/self/status</span>, extracts the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">TracerPid</span> line, converts it to an integer, and, if greater than zero, appends a formatted key/value into the request body via the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">b.so</span>’s string‑builder.</li>
<li>A staging helper concatenates an existing buffer with a pseudo‑random block derived from an input string:
<ul>
<li>It seeds a byte with <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">rand()</span></li>
<li>It XORs each subsequent byte of the input into a rolling accumulator</li>
<li>It writes the accumulator bytes as a suffix</li>
<li>It then writes the combined buffer to a given file path via the <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">b.so</span>’s writer</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>A two‑step installer/uninstaller pair uses three config keys: <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">persistency_origin</span>, <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">persistency_payload</span> and <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">persistency_backup</span>. The main routine checks that all three are set, copies the backup back to the origin if needed and then deletes the payload file. It returns distinct status codes (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x4B</span>/<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x4C</span>/<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x4D</span>) that map to the message catalog entries for “no config,” “failed move” and “failed unlink.” A sibling routine conditionally creates or truncates the backup file (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">fopen</span> with mode “<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">w</span>”) when a global persistence flag is set.</li>
<li>Battery percentage via <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sysfs</span>: A utility reads battery capacity from the system’s power‑supply <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">sysfs</span>, checking two common locations: <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/sys/class/power_supply/battery/capacity</span> and <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/sys/class/power_supply/Battery/capacity</span>.</li>
<li>Two routines set up and finalize the working directory under app‑private storage.
<ul>
<li>The first creates the directory tree, applies mode <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0771</span> (<span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">0x1F9</span>), temporarily adds execute to the parent and copies the resolved path into <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">config</span>. And, when running as <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">root</span>, it attempts to mount a <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">tmpfs</span> at that location to keep artifacts in memory</li>
<li>The second (cleanup/finalize) can, when root and the directory exists, run <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">lsof | grep &lt;working_dir&gt;</span> and ship the result home. It then restores the parent directory’s original mode and frees the path buffer</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Process discovery by SELinux context and by <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">cmdline</span>: Two search helpers iterate <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/proc</span>, building and reading per‑PID files.
<ul>
<li>One compares <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/proc/%d/attr/current</span> against a target SELinux context and then confirms the process has PPID 1</li>
<li>The other compares <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">/proc/%d/cmdline</span> against a target <span style="font-family: 'courier new', courier, monospace;">cmdline</span></li>
<li>On a match, they write the PID to an out‑parameter and return success</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Debug‑printing a variant array: A developer‑facing routine prints a small typed array structure. It formats type names from a table, dumps short byte arrays inside square brackets and emits a single character for a specific type, one element per line. This looks like leftover debugging and is not invoked by active code.</li>
</ul>
<p>None of these helpers are exercised by this component’s main execution loop. Their presence is consistent with a staged architecture in which subsequently loaded shared objects, forming the complete LANDFALL framework, expand collection and persistence using capabilities already compiled into this loader.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
      
              </div>

<script>

(function($) {
	// Migrated from the unit42-v5 + Modifications
	var subscribeSuccess = false;
	var email = document.getElementById('newsletter-email');
	var subscription_form = document.getElementById('unit42footerSubscription_form');
	var subscription_form_button = document.getElementById('unit42footerSubscription_form_button');
	window.captchaComplete = function() {
		subscribeSuccess = true;
		if ($(mail).val() != '' && isEmail($(mail).val())) {
			$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-disabled');
		}
		setTimeout(function() {
			$(email).focus();
			$('.g-recaptcha iframe').attr('tabindex', '-1');
		}, 100)
	}
	window.captchaExpires = function() {
		subscribeSuccess = false;
		$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled', true);
	} 
	$(subscription_form).submit(function(e) {
		e.preventDefault();
		e.stopImmediatePropagation();
		updateEmailMask();
		var success = true;
		var form = $(this);
		var mail = form.find('input[name="Email"]');
		
		if (mail.val() === '') {
			mail.addClass('has-error');
			showError(1);
			success = false;	
		}  else if (!isEmail(mail.val())){
			showError(2);
			success = false;
		}
		else {
			mail.removeClass('has-error');
			$('.error-mail').addClass('d-none');
		}

		if (!subscribeSuccess) {
			$('.error-recaptcha').removeClass('d-none');
		} else {
			$('.error-recaptcha').addClass('d-none');
		}

		if (success && subscribeSuccess) {
			$.ajax({
				type: 'POST',
				url: form.attr('action'),
				data: form.serialize(),
				beforeSend: function() {
                 form.find('button').addClass('is-loading');
    			},
				success: function(msg) {
					form.find('.form-success-message').html('<p class="success-message">You have been successfully subscribed</p>');
					form.find('button').removeClass('is-loading');
					$(email).val('');
					clearError();
				},
				error: function(jqXHR, textStatus, errorThrown) {
					$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled', true);
					form.find('button').removeClass('is-loading');
					
				}
			});
		}
		return false;
	});
	function showError(error_type){
		if(error_type == 1) {
			$('.error-mail').text("Please enter the email address.").addClass('error-show');
			$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled');
		} else if(error_type == 2){
			$('.error-mail').text("Please provide a valid e-mail address.").addClass('error-show');
			$(subscription_form_button).addClass('is-disabled');
		}	
		$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-loading');
	}
	function clearError(){
		$('.error-mail').text("").removeClass('error-show');;
		$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-loading');
		$(subscription_form_button).removeClass('is-disabled');
	}
	
	$(email).on('input', function (event) { 
		var email = $(this).val();
		if (isEmail(email) ) {			
			clearError();
		} else if(email == ""){
			clearError();
		} else{
			showError(2);			  
		}
    });

	function isEmail(email) {
		var re = /^(([^<>()\[\]\\.,;:\s@"]+(\.[^<>()\[\]\\.,;:\s@"]+)*)|(".+"))@((\[[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\.[0-9]{1,3}\])|(([a-zA-Z\-0-9]+\.)+[a-zA-Z]{2,}))$/;
		return re.test(String(email).toLowerCase());
	}
	
	var captcha_loaded = false;
         if(!captcha_loaded){
            // recaptcha on foucs call
            $(document).on('change paste keyup', '#newsletter-email', function () {
               if($('.g-recaptcha').hasClass('d-none')){
                $('.g-recaptcha').removeClass('d-none');
               }
               if(!captcha_loaded ){
                  captcha_loaded  = true;
                  // trigger loading api.js (recaptcha.js) script
                  var head = document.getElementsByTagName('head')[0];
                  var script = document.createElement('script');
                  script.type = 'text/javascript';
                  script.src = 'https://www.google.com/recaptcha/api.js?hl=en_US';
                  head.appendChild(script);
               }
            });
        }
	
	function updateEmailMask() {
		var email = $("#unit42footerSubscription_form input[name='Email']").val();
		if (email && email.trim() != '') {
			var maskedEmail = maskEmailAddress(email);
			$("#unit42footerSubscription_form input[name='emailFormMask']").val(maskedEmail);
		}
	}

	function maskEmailAddress (emailAddress) {
		function mask(str) {
			var strLen = str.length;
			if (strLen > 4) {
				return str.substr(0, 1) + str.substr(1, strLen - 1).replace(/\w/g, '*') + str.substr(-1,1);
			}
			return str.replace(/\w/g, '*');
		}
		return emailAddress.replace(/([\w.]+)@([\w.]+)(\.[\w.]+)/g, function (m, p1, p2, p3) {
			return mask(p1) + '@' + mask(p2) + p3;
		});

		return emailAddress;
	}

}(jQuery));
//# sourceMappingURL=main.js.map
	
</script>

	$(document).ready(function(){
		$("html").on("click", ".language-btn", function () {
			$(this).toggleClass("expand");
			$(".language-dropdown").toggleClass("expand");
		});

		$("html").on("click", "body", function (b) {
			if (!$(b.target).is(".language-btn")) {
				$(".language-btn").removeClass("expand");
				$(".language-dropdown").removeClass("expand");
			}
		});
	});

    function alter_ul_post_values(obj,post_id,ul_type){
	
		if (isProcessing)    
		return;  
		isProcessing = true;   
		var like_nonce = jQuery('#_wpnonce').val();
		jQuery(obj).find("span").html("..");
                jQuery.ajax({
                    type: "POST",
                    url: "https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/wp-content/plugins/like-dislike-counter-for-posts-pages-and-comments/ajax_counter.php",
                    data: "post_id="+post_id+"&up_type="+ul_type+"&ul_nonce="+like_nonce,
                    success: function(msg){
                            jQuery(obj).find("span").html(msg);
                            isProcessing = false; 
                            jQuery(obj).find('svg').children('path').attr('stroke','#0050FF');
                            jQuery(obj).removeClass('idc_ul_cont_not_liked idc_ul_cont_not_liked_inner');

    script1.setAttribute('src',url);
    if(defer == true){
        script1.setAttribute('defer','defer');
    }
    document.head.appendChild(script1);
}
function loadScript1(url, callback){

    var script = document.createElement("script")
    script.type = "text/javascript";
    if (script.readyState){  //IE
        script.onreadystatechange = function(){
            if (script.readyState == "loaded" || script.readyState == "complete"){
                script.onreadystatechange = null;
                callback();
            }
        };
    } else {  //Others
        script.onload = function(){
            callback();
        };
    }
    script.src = url;
    document.getElementsByTagName("head")[0].appendChild(script);
}
if(referer == "CloudCortex" || referer == "Prisma" || referer == "Cortex" || referer == "Sase" || referer == "Unit" || referer == "Ngfw"){

      loadScript(main_site_criticalTopBase, false);
      loadScript1(main_site_criticalTopProductNav, function(){
        window.PAN_initializeProduct2021Nav();
      });
      loadScript(main_site_defered, false);
    }, 3000);
	}
	else{
    setTimeout(function(){
      loadScript1(main_site_critical_top, function(){
        window.PAN_initializeProduct2021Nav();
      });
      loadScript(main_site_defered, false);
    }, 3000);
	}
}

document.addEventListener("DOMContentLoaded", function() {
  // Find all the forms on the page
  var forms = document.getElementsByTagName("form");

  // Loop through each form
  for (var i = 0; i < forms.length; i++) {
    var form = forms[i];

    // Check if the hidden input field already exists
    var requestInput = form.querySelector("input[name='web_unit42_req_id']");
    if (!requestInput) {
      // Create a hidden input element if it doesn't exist
      requestInput = document.createElement("input");
      requestInput.type = "hidden";
      requestInput.name = "web_unit42_req_id";
      requestInput.id = "web_unit42_req_id";

      // Append the hidden field to the form
      form.appendChild(requestInput);
    }
  }
});

// Assign the onCheqResponse function to the window object to handle events from any form
window.onCheqResponse = function(encryptedMessage, requestID) {
  // Find all the forms on the page
  var forms = document.getElementsByTagName("form");

  for (var i = 0; i < forms.length; i++) {
    var form = forms[i];
    var requestInput = form.querySelector("input[name='web_unit42_req_id']");
    if (requestInput) {
      requestInput.value = requestID;
    }
  }
};
